# Approval register

Every item needing the user's decision is recorded here with a stable
identifier. Read this file before proposing anything, so settled questions are
not reopened.

This is Juna's register. It is governed by `SOP_HUMAN_AI_COWORK.md`.

- One identifier records one decision.
- Identifiers are never reused.
- A rejected item keeps its identifier.
- Silence is not approval.

Next free Juna identifier: **JCM-355**. JCM-080 through JCM-152 are
unavailable in this branch because they are already allocated or reserved in
the reconciled register. The paper-audit decisions initially presented with
colliding identifiers are recorded without collision as JCM-153 through
JCM-165 below.
JCM-253 remains reserved for the matched Blue-28 campaign.

The earlier D sequence still has a gap. D6 to D12 were approved in another
session but were never recorded. They remain reserved and must not be reused.

| JCM-061 | approved | Replace the 548-word abstract in `JunaCore/juna_joint_ieee.tex` with this 202-word abstract: "Underwater acoustic communication channels are time varying and frequency selective. Multipath and ambient noise make uncoded symbol decisions unreliable, so transmissions carry a low-density parity-check (LDPC) code whose parity constraints couple every coded carrier across the packet. The separate receiver uses pilot-trained combiner weights to form pre-decoder soft symbols from partial fast Fourier transform (Partial-FFT) observations before LDPC decoding. However, where pilots are sparse and residual Doppler strong, separating demodulation from decoding discards the information the code carries during the combiner fit. We formulate one objective over the Partial-FFT response, combiner, and relaxed codeword bits; observations, pilots, and parity constraints regularize one another. This joint maximum a posteriori (MAP) problem is intractable because bits are discrete while the response and combiner are continuous. We therefore relax coded bits to real variables and replace parity checks with differentiable penalties. We show that completeness and exactness trade against each other; the fewer variables the gradient moves, the more of the objective is solved in closed form. Although we do not claim that the estimated response is the physical intercarrier interference, different responses can reproduce the same observations. All receivers use the same Partial-FFT observations; the code acts during symbol estimation rather than after it." This is a deletion-based condensation of lines 18--68. It removes the receiver-by-receiver claims about the pilot-only recovery, decoder-posterior fit, bit-only response solve, and joint-step acceptance rule; preserves the CL-58 channel-to-code and discarded-code-information chain; introduces no technical claim or name; and does not resolve the separate open decisions CL-46, CL-48, CL-49, or CL-51. User: "061 approved". |

Section IV then continues unchanged through the ridge criterion, batch solution, recursive update, and information-loss close. Codex identifiers use the `CX` prefix. Next free Codex identifier: **CX-95**.

| CX-1 | proposed | Create a separate source `juna_joint_ieee_complexity.tex` and its compiled PDF, titled `Complexity of the Five Receiver Policies`. Leave `juna_joint_ieee.tex` unchanged. The file name follows the paper file name; every title word comes from the user's request or the current paper. |
| CX-2 | proposed | State warm per-frame decode time and working storage separately from cached code construction. Retain the paper's symbols $N$, $B$, $M$, $K_{\mathrm{ICI}}$, $n_b$, $n_{\mathrm{par}}$, and $\mathcal N_j$. Define $N_{\mathrm{act}}=|\Kset|$, $N_{\mathrm{pil}}=|\Pset|$, $N_{\mathrm{band}}$, $Q=2K_{\mathrm{ICI}}+1$, $E=\sum_j|\mathcal N_j|$, the BP/refinement/trial counts, $N_{\mathrm{sync}}$, and $L_{\mathrm{rx}}$. Separate the shared observation cost, one decoder checkpoint, and the conditional-$C$, objective-and-gradient, initialization, and accepted-state checkpoint costs before specializing them to the five receivers. |
| CX-3 | proposed | Derive OFDM+LDPC from the executable frame path as the shared $M$-view FFT preparation, view summation, residual pilot equalization, metric formation, and one BP decode. Do not claim that this implementation computes only one FFT: all five public frame paths first compute $M$ length-$N$ FFTs per block. |
| CX-4 | proposed | Derive Partial-FFT+LDPC as the shared preparation plus, for every block and frequency band, accumulation of an $M\times M$ pilot normal matrix, an $O(M^3)$ linear solve, application of $M$ weights to the active carriers, residual pilot equalization, and one BP decode. State the all-pilot fallback cost for a band with fewer than $M$ local pilots. |
| CX-5 | proposed | Derive JUNA-Lite as one complete Partial-FFT candidate plus zero, one, or two completed posterior-anchor combiner refits and BP checkpoints. For the deployed zero threshold and nonbinding anchor limit, include every available data carrier in each completed fit. State the early exits on candidate validity or lack of ordered improvement. |
| CX-6 | proposed | Derive Profiled $C,z$ conditionally: a passing Standard CRC returns at Standard cost; a failed gate pays the outer Standard decode, a repeated Standard decode inside the rescue, JUNA-Lite, initialization, and eight steps. Each reported step performs two conditional bandwise $C$ solves, two derived-$W$ updates, two full objective-and-gradient sweeps, and one BP checkpoint. Record the repeated Standard decode as executable work, even though the paper-level algorithm describes one Standard formation. |
| CX-7 | proposed | Derive Direct $C,z$ conditionally: a passing Standard CRC returns at Standard cost; after failure, each of eight steps performs a current-state objective-and-gradient sweep, between one and thirteen backtracking trial sweeps, and a BP checkpoint only after an accepted step. State that first-trial acceptance can make Direct cheaper than Profiled, while the Direct backtracking worst case can be larger; there is no unconditional ordering between them. |
| CX-8 | proposed | Report the controlled paper-geometry timing at source commit `99bfd13be42e0cd49506e9eb0bc832d6268462f4`: one deterministic 1926-bit, five-block, $N=1024$, $M=4$, six-band, CRC-16 frame at 1.3 dB AWGN and seed 211, decoded from the identical 9536 received samples with Julia and BLAS each pinned to one thread. After one warm-up, three rotated timings give medians OFDM+LDPC 37.69 ms, Partial-FFT+LDPC 45.51 ms, JUNA-Lite 73.03 ms, Direct $C,z$ 218.52 ms, and Profiled $C,z$ 277.53 ms. Traces prove Standard CRC failure, both Lite refits, eight Profiled steps, and eight accepted Direct steps. Say that this frame supports the predicted conditional order but does not prove asymptotic scaling or a universal order over frames. |

The information-theoretic framing matches the commented block of `reference papers/tex/gab.tex` (data-processing inequality, mutual-information loss), which the body can later state formally. |

The introduction's problem paragraph now tells it in prose: a carrier is a clock making a whole number of turns; a bin reads by spinning backward and averaging; orthogonality is loop closure; residual Doppler leaves every loop a fraction short, and the leftover arrow is the ICI coefficient. The derivation in the OFDM section points at a new two-panel figure (fig:loops): closed loop with zero average versus unfinished loop with the leftover arrow equal to H_{k,l}. | CL-116 | open | Survey finding, attribution: eq:derived-weights is the perfect-channel-knowledge MMSE combiner of Yerramalli, Stojanovic and Mitra (already cited as yerramalli), Section III-A, specialized to a single response column. Proposal: derive eq:derived-weights in one line from that criterion, cite Section III-A, and state the one difference — the joint paper conditions on the estimated Partial-FFT response rather than on the true channel. |

Proposal: cite it where the paper contrasts separate demodulation and decoding, and name it as the comparison baseline. A reviewer will produce it otherwise. |
| CL-118 | open | Survey finding, support for the framing: J. M. Walsh, P. A. Regalia and C. R. Johnson, "Turbo Decoding as Iterative Constrained Maximum-Likelihood Sequence Detection", IEEE Trans. Inf. Theory 52(12), 2006, shows the turbo decoder is a block Gauss-Seidel iteration zeroing the gradient of a Lagrangian for a constrained maximum-likelihood problem. Proposal: cite it in the general section — it is the precedent for naming the optimization problem that an iterative receiver approximates, which is the move the paper makes. |
| CL-119 | open | Survey finding, the nearest ancestor of the second receiver: Y. M. Aval and M. Stojanovic, "Differentially Coherent Multichannel Detection of Acoustic OFDM Signals", IEEE JOE 40(2), 2015, learns the multiple-FFT combiner weights by scaled stochastic gradient (or RLS across carriers) on the post-detection mean squared error, and freezes the weights whenever the error or the gradient exceeds a threshold. Two differences to state: the adaptation is driven by uncoded decisions, and the authors keep forward error correction out of the loop on purpose — "the proposed techniques do not require repetition of FEC decoding" (Section IV-A). Proposal: cite it where the paper introduces searching the combiner weights, so the reader sees which part is prior art and which part is the code entering the loop. |

The shared noun is now used deliberately where it pays: the identity paragraph reads "The M per-view coefficients of a carrier pair therefore sum to its channel coefficient: the two are the same quantity at two resolutions", and the Fig. 3 caption matches. | CL-210 | open | Proposed Fig. 2 caption (replaces the current six sentences; drops the N-samples definition, which the notation table carries, and merges the pilot definitions): "Frame and code layout.  The time-domain frame has a synchronization signal at each end and a sequence of CP-OFDM blocks, each carrying outer pilots every \(O\) active carriers, QPSK data carriers with two coded bits each, and fixed filler symbols.  One frame-wide code maps the message bits --- payload, cyclic redundancy check (CRC), and one fixed inner-pilot bit every \(I\) message positions --- to the \(n_b\) coded bits across all blocks, with \(n_{\mathrm{par}}\) parity bits; outer pilots are known carrier symbols, and inner pilots are fixed message positions whose metrics are clamped before belief propagation (BP).  The Tanner-graph panel illustrates these variable classes with one identity edge per parity variable; its displayed message edges are not indexed rows of \(\mathbf P\), and the codebook \eqref{eq:codebook} requires only \(\mathbf P\mathbf b^{T}=\mathbf 0\)." |

| CL-267 | result, negative | The guard question raised by the 30 dB delay spans is answered, and the answer is negative. At identical geometry (1024 8/8), doubling the cyclic prefix from 64 to 128 leaves error-free frames essentially unchanged - OFDM+LDPC 2891 to 2886 of 6144, JUNA-C,z 3772 to 3785, JUNA-Iterative 3509 to 3557, all under half a percent - while the payload falls from 1926 to 1538 bits per frame, so the configured effective rate drops about 20 percent for every receiver (OFDM+LDPC -20.3, Partial-FFT -15.8, JUNA-Iterative -19.1, JUNA-C,z -19.9, JUNA-Direct-C,z -20.2). Partial-FFT is the only receiver that gains frames at all (1949 to 2055, about 5 percent) and it still loses rate. Hence the delay energy beyond CP64 is not what limits these paths: lengthening the guard to cover more of the 30 dB span costs a fifth of the payload and buys no frames. This bears directly on the design-consequence paragraph, which currently only observes that the spans exceed the admitted prefixes; it can now say that extending the prefix was tested and did not pay. The CP256 rung queued in CL-264 will show whether the loss continues linearly. |

At 512 5/5 CP64 JUNA-C,z (3385) trails OFDM+LDPC (4076); at CP128 it overtakes it (4188 against 4097). The honest statement is that the prefix trade depends on FFT length and receiver, not that lengthening it never pays. |

Within-capture permutation p is 0.175 to 0.939 for the receiver effect, so the hydrophone ordering inside a capture carries nothing: the evidence is four effective points, not twelve. | CL-304 | result | User: "how about the tables in Juna_joint_ieee.pdf? do you have any ideas about better visualization?" Survey of the seventeen tables led to a reporting finding in the three timing tables that matters more than the visualization question. THE FINDING: JUNA-C,z warm decode time is bimodal over the 216 configuration-path cells of `figures/red_one_frame_timing_evidence/red_one_frame_timing.csv` - 125 cells below 200 ms with median 66.5 and 91 cells at or above with median 328.2, and the largest gaps in the sorted times sit well outside that range, so the split is clean. The mechanism is the policy's own condition, not the channel: 124 of the 125 fast cells have `selection_reason=standard_crc_valid` and stop after one decode, while 86 of the 91 slow cells have `rescue_executed=true` and pay a second solve. CONSEQUENCE FOR TABLES XIII TO XV. Table XIII prints median 81.854 ms with Q1-Q3 of 65.063 to 311.289 and a ratio of 1.26 for JUNA-C,z; that interval straddles the gap between the two modes, so no frame takes about 82 ms and the ratio describes nothing that runs. Table XV, median by path, is worse: the per-path slow-branch share runs 5.6 to 83.3 percent, and because the median lands in the empty region between the modes it converts that share into a published 5x spread - red 4 h1 and red 4 h3 have the identical share of 8 slow cells in 18, yet print 80.0 and 199.8 ms. So the by-path table reports how often the standard CRC failed on that path, expressed as if it were a decode time. Table XIV, by configuration, has the same defect with a real ordering underneath it: the slow share rises with the FFT length, from 0 of 12 at N=512 5/5 to 10 of 12 at N=4096 10/5. Table XIII already carries the branch counts, 8 joint candidate and 79 standard fallback, in two columns beside the median, so the paper holds both halves and never connects them. WHAT THE FIGURE SHOULD BE: one strip of all 216 cells per receiver on a log time axis, which makes the two modes and the empty region visible at once, with the slow-branch share as a second panel ordered by FFT length; that is the honest replacement, and the median-and-quartile summary should either go or be reported per branch. OTHER TABLES, ranked by what a figure would add. The three largest-change tables are entirely "before to after" pairs and are a slopegraph written out in arrows. Table VIII, the four ranked choices per path, is a ranked dot chart in which the red 4 swap becomes a visible crossing. The thirty-nine configuration table, the notation table, the evidence-set table and the operation-count table are lookup and should stay as tables. No file was edited. |

| CL-307 | result | User: "do some analysis to uncover certain class of channel ... where juna or ofdm+fec would work. or outer pilots or inner pilots would improve a lot, or Nc would improve a lot, or CP would. find those associations." Built the widest grid available in the session scratchpad: 40 configurations by 12 paths by 5 receivers by 16 SNR values, 38400 cells, from the committed `red_configuration_curves.py` generator for the 22 CP64 configurations plus the retained aggregates in `red_configuration_curves_evidence` for the rest; spans N 512 to 4096, CP 64/128/256, outer spacing 4 to 40, inner spacing 5 to 40. Five design levers were formed as signed single-change differences in the configured one-second effective payload rate, averaged over every valid pair, receiver and SNR value on each path: denser outer pilots (20 configuration pairs), denser inner pilots (23), longer FFT (44), longer CP (5), and each receiver against OFDM+LDPC over all configurations. HEADLINE: each lever is predicted by the channel dimension it acts in, and the three families separate cleanly. Against the common phase rate, the 30 dB delay span, and the phase-removed off-diagonal fraction respectively, the strongest predictor is: outer pilots -> delay span rho=+0.855, inner pilots -> delay span +0.851, longer CP -> delay span +0.872, longer FFT -> phase-removed off-diagonal +0.832 (its drift correlation is +0.007, essentially nil), JUNA-C,z -> drift +0.937, JUNA-Iterative -> drift +0.923, Partial-FFT -> drift +0.797. So the delay-domain levers follow delay spread, the block-length lever follows within-block tap change, and every receiver lever follows drift. CLASSES. Split on the raw current-block off-diagonal fraction at 0.148 gives {red 1, red 2} against {red 3, red 4} with no overlap: above it the Partial-FFT front end gains +0.0021 bit/s/Hz and below it LOSES 0.0314, while JUNA-C,z gains +0.0402 above and still +0.0180 below and JUNA-Iterative +0.0336 against +0.0052. So the partial-FFT front end is the lever that changes sign, and OFDM+LDPC is the right default on the low-drift class. Split on the 30 dB delay span at 24.1 ms: denser inner pilots net +0.0006 above and -0.0036 below. Denser outer pilots are positive on every red 1 to red 3 path (+0.0023 to +0.0163) and negative on all three red 4 paths (-0.0016 to -0.0022). Longer FFT is negative on all twelve paths (-0.0128 to -0.0279) and longer CP is negative on all twelve (-0.0029 to -0.0155). FOUR FENCES. (1) Every lever also costs payload, so these gains are net of it: denser outer pilots -8.1 percent, denser inner pilots -5.3, longer FFT -9.1, longer CP -12.5 percent of payload bits per frame; the outer-pilot result is therefore a real recovery gain that outpays its cost, while the inner-pilot and CP results may be the cost alone. (2) The CP lever rests on only five configuration pairs, all at N=512 or 1024 and spacings 5/5 or 8/8, so its sign is not established. (3) Drift and delay span themselves correlate at rho=+0.70 over the twelve paths, so the families are not independent; the separation argument rests on the contrast in rho per lever, notably the +0.937 against +0.620 for JUNA and the +0.007 against +0.490 for FFT length, and not on orthogonal descriptors. (4) Twelve paths are four capture blocks, so the smallest attainable capture-block p is 0.0417, which several of these reach. Scripts in the session scratchpad; no file in any repository was edited. |

The Blue CFO-fix queue at 261a441 halted at config 2/19 with "blue3_hydrophone1 30 dB OFDM BER is 0.21658761160714285, expected below 0.1". Config 1/19 (n512-cp64-r025-p6-8) completed and validated first - max_30db_ofdm_ber=0.014505854, all 12 path contracts matching their traces by sha256 - which restored the nine contracts an earlier interrupted run had deleted at its Remove-Item stage. The failure is physical, not a harness or validator artifact: the gate reads one row and 6210/28672 sums byte-exactly from the frame trace, and diagnose_blue_sparse_pilots.jl, which calls benchmark_frame_capture directly and bypasses both the density runner and the validator, reproduces it at 0.21620 (6199/28672, 6/32 frames) while blue2 lane 1 returns exactly 0.0 (32/32) under the identical code path. The cause is the channel, not the configuration. At n512 spacing 20, 30 dB, seed 4, 32 frames: blue2 h1 0.0 32/32 (reproduced across two processes), blue4 h1/h2/h3 all 0.0 32/32 with zero bit errors of 28672, against blue3 h1 0.21620 6/32. Three of four captures are flawless exactly where blue3 loses 26 of 32 frames; blue3 carries both the largest delay spread (50.70 grid samples against blue2 40.02 and blue4 34.02) and the worst intra-frame carrier drift (to 12.1 Hz against 9.54 Hz subcarrier spacing), the only capture worst on both axes. Measured curve for blue3 h1, spacing swept at n512: 3.887e-5 (31/32), 0.09469 (20/32), 0.14266 (14/32), 0.21620 (6/32) at spacings 10, 14, 16, 20; across FFT length at spacing 20: 0.21620 (512), 0.06582 (1024), 0.22153 (1536); and 0.05134 at 1024 spacing 10. Only n512 spacing 10 is genuinely clean. Three mechanistic models were proposed and all three are falsified by these numbers, so the record is the measurements, not a mechanism: the N/D alias-free-window model predicted a cliff at spacing 16 (degradation is smooth and starts between 10 and 14); window-equivalence predicted n512 spacing 10 and n1024 spacing 20 would match at window 51.2 (they differ by a factor of 1700); and larger nfft was predicted to mitigate spacing 20 (n1536 at 0.22153 is worse than n1024 and matches n512, so the dependence is non-monotone in nfft). Consequence of the uniform gate: config 2 (n512-d10-p20) and config 10 (n1536-d10-p20) are genuine negative results for blue3 and the 19-config queue cannot complete as written; configs 3-19 were never started. Note also that d20-p10 and d30-p7 hold pre-CFO-fix state (blue2/3/4 at 0.477-0.498, 0/32 frames) and have no markers, so they are unmeasured rather than passing. Evidence committed on branch agent/blue-cfofix-rerun-261a441 (67c40b4) as results, not as accepted results. Sweep script generalised to JunaCore/bench/diagnose_blue_pilot_spacing_sweep.jl, which takes nfft:spacing pairs through JUNA_BLUE_CONFIGS and channel:lane paths as ARGS; the parent diagnose_blue_sparse_pilots.jl hardcodes both and is left untouched. Both sit in bench/, which JunaCore/.gitignore excludes, so like every diagnostic there they are on disk but not tracked - only the four n512_crc_no_harm pipeline files in that directory are. The numbers above are therefore the durable record; regenerating them needs the script re-derived from its parent if the working copy is lost. |
